Thereupon Kaplun ordered the group set free and consented that I give them of the bread sent for the workers’ rations. But a valuable day was lost.
The next morning a group of boys and girls came singing along the Nevski Prospekt. They were *kursanti* from the Tauride Palace who were sent to my office to work. On my first visit to the palace I had been shown the quarters of the *kursanti*, the students of the Bolshevik academy. They were mostly village boys and girls housed, fed, clothed, and educated by the Government, later to be placed in responsible positions in the Soviet regime.

===Chinese===

原来都在从前的娼妓区；都是些下等窑子，直到布尔什维克关闭一切妓院。房子被蛀蚀殆尽，臭气熏天，污秽不堪。要在这两星期里把这些黑洞变成像样的住处，绝非小难事。刷层漆？想都别想，那是奢侈。只有一条路：把屋里的家具帷幄清空，彻底打扫消毒。

一天早晨，一伙形容凄惨的人被两名民兵押到我临时的办公室，说是派来干活的。这一伙人：一个独臂老头、一个痨病女人、八个男孩女孩——十足的孩子，苍白、饥饿、衣衫褴褛。「这些不幸的人从哪儿来？」我问。「投机客，」一个民兵答，「在市场上抓的。」囚人们哭了起来，他们不是投机客，他们抗议说；他们饿，两天没领到面包了，是被逼到市场上卖火柴、卖线头换口面包。正说着，老头饿晕了过去——比言语更好地证明：他投机的只是饥饿。这样的「投机客」我先前见过：一队队被押过莫斯科和彼得格勒街头，押送队的枪口顶着囚徒的后背。

我不能让这些饿鬼来做这活。可民兵坚持不放人：奉命让他们干活。我打电话给卡普隆，告诉他：让俄国囚徒——他们唯一的罪名是饥饿——来为美国被逐者准备住处，恕我不能同意。卡普隆随即下令放人，并同意我把发给工人口粮的面包分给他们。可宝贵的一天又损失了。

第二天早晨，一队男孩女孩唱着歌沿涅瓦大街走来——塔夫利宫的*学员队*，被派到我办公室来干活。我初次参观塔夫利宫时看过*学员队*的营房，那是布尔什维克干校的学员，多为农村青年，由政府供食宿、供教育，日后安插到苏维埃政权的负责岗位上。

===Original===

At the time I was impressed by the institutions, but by April I had looked somewhat beneath the surface. I recalled what a young woman, a Communist, had told me in Moscow about these students. “They are the special caste now being reared in Russia,” she had said. “Like the church which maintains and educates its religious priesthood, our Government trains a military and civic priesthood. They are a favoured lot.” I had more than one occasion to convince myself of the truth of it. The *kursanti* were being given every advantage and many special privileges. They knew their importance and they behaved accordingly.
Their first demand when they came to me was for the extra rations of bread they had been promised. This demand satisfied, they stood about and seemed to have no idea of work. It was evident that whatever else the *kursanti* might be taught, it was not to labour. But, then, few people in Russia know how to work. The situation looked hopeless. Only ten days remained till the arrival of the deportees, and the “hotels” assigned for their use were still in as uninhabitable a condition as before. It was no use to threaten with the Cheka, as my co-workers did. I appealed to the boys and girls in the spirit of the American deportees who were about to arrive in Russia full of enthusiasm for the Revolution and eager to join in the great work of reconstruction. The *kursanti* were the pampered charges of the Government, but they were not long from the villages, and they had had no time to become corrupt. My appeal was effective. They took up the work with a will, and at the end of ten days the three famous hotels were ready as far as willingness to work and hot water without soap could make them. We were very proud of our achievement and we eagerly awaited the arrival of the deportees.
At last they came, but to our great surprise they proved to be no deportees at all. They were Russian war prisoners from Germany. The misunderstanding was due to the blunder of some official in Chicherin’s office who misread the radio information about the party due at the border. The prepared hotels were locked and sealed; they were not to be used for the returned war prisoners because “they were prepared for American deportees who still might come.” All the efforts and labour had been in vain.

===Chinese===

当时我对这类机构印象不坏，但到四月，我已看出了些门道。我想起莫斯科一位女共产党对我讲过这些学生的话。「他们是如今俄国正在豢养的特权种姓，」她说，「如同教会供养教育它的宗教祭司，我们的政府训练的是军事与民政的祭司。他们是得宠的一群。」这话的真实性，我不止一次得到印证。*学员队*享有一切便利和许多特权，他们知道自己的分量，举止也与之相称。

他们到我跟前的第一个要求，是兑现许诺给他们的加份面包。满足了这一条，他们就站着晃悠，全无干活的概念。显然，*学员队*什么都学，就是不学劳动。不过话说回来，俄国也没几个人会干活。局面看着无望：距被逐者抵达只剩十天，而拨给他们用的那几家「旅馆」还是老样子，无法住人。学同事们拿契卡吓人，没用。我换了个调子向男孩女孩们呼吁：即将来俄的美国被逐者，个个满腔革命热忱，急着投身重建大业。*学员队*虽是政府的娇儿，可他们离开村子不久，还没来得及学坏。我的呼吁奏了效。他们劲头十足地干起来，十天下来，那三家著名的旅馆，凭着一股肯干和没有肥皂的热水，总算收拾像样了。我们很为自己的成就骄傲，急切地等着被逐者到来。

他们终于来了——可令我们大吃一惊，来的根本不是被逐者，而是从德国回来的俄国战俘。误会出在契切林办公室某官员的失误，他看错了关于即将抵境者的无线电通报。备好的旅馆上了锁、贴了封条——不能给归国战俘用，因为「那是为可能还会来的美国被逐者准备的」。全部努力与辛劳，付诸东流。

## VII Rest Homes for Workers / 七、工人休养之家
===Original===

Since my return from Moscow I noticed a change in Zorin’s attitude: he was reserved, distant, and not as friendly as when we first met. I ascribed it to the fact that he was overworked and fatigued, and not wishing to waste his valuable time I ceased visiting the Zorins as frequently as before. One day, however, he called up to ask if Alexander Berkman and myself would join him in certain work he was planning, and which was to be done in hurry-up American style, as he put it. On calling to see him we found him rather excited﻿—an unusual thing for Zorin who was generally quiet and reserved. He was full of a new scheme to build “rest homes” for workers. He explained that on Kameniy Ostrov were the magnificent mansions of the Stolypins, the Polovtsovs, and others of the aristocracy and bourgeoisie, and that he was planning to turn them into recreation centres for workers. Would we join in the work? Of course, we consented eagerly, and the next morning we went over to inspect the island. It was indeed an ideal spot, dotted with magnificent mansions, some of them veritable museums, containing rare gems of painting, tapestry, and furniture. The man in charge of the buildings called our attention to the art treasures, protesting that they would be injured or entirely destroyed if put to the planned use. But Zorin was set on his scheme. “Recreation homes for workers are more important than art,” he said.
We returned to the Astoria determined to devote ourselves to the work and to go at it intensively, as the houses were to be ready for the First of May. We prepared detailed plans for dining rooms, sleeping chambers, reading rooms, theatre and lecture halls, and recreation places for the workers. As the first and most necessary step we proposed the organization of a dining room to feed the workers who were to be employed in preparing the place for their comrades. I had learned from my previous experience with the hotels that much valuable time was lost because of the failure to provide for those actually employed on such work. Zorin consented and promised that we were to take charge within a few days. But a week passed and nothing further was heard about what was to be a rush job. Some time later Zorin called up to ask us to accompany him to the island. On our arrival there we found half-a-dozen Commissars already in charge, with scores of people idling about.

===Chinese===

从莫斯科回来后，我察觉佐林的态度变了：矜持、疏远，不似初见时友好。我把它归因于他操劳过度，不愿再耗费他的宝贵时间，便不似先前那样常去佐林家了。可是有一天他来电话，问亚历山大·伯克曼和我可愿参加他正在筹划的一项工作——照他的说法，要按美国式的快节奏干。去见他时，我们发现他相当兴奋——这对素来沉静寡言的佐林可是新鲜事。他满脑子新计划：为工人建「休养之家」。他解释说，卡缅内岛上尽是斯托雷平家、波尔佐佐夫家等贵族资产阶级的华厦，他计划把它们改成工人的娱乐中心。我们愿不愿意参加？我们当然踊跃应允，第二天一早就去察看了那座岛。那真是块宝地，华厦星罗棋布，有几座简直像博物馆，藏着绘画、织毯、家具的珍品。管房子的人提醒我们注意那些艺术珍藏，抗议说若按计划改作他用，它们必被损毁或荡然无存。可佐林铁了心。「工人的娱乐之家比艺术更要紧。」他说。

我们回到阿斯托里亚，决意全力以赴赶这工程——房子必须在五一前备好。我们为餐厅、寝室、阅览室、剧场和讲堂、游乐场拟了详细的方案。第一步也是最要紧的一步，我们提议先办个食堂，供那些为同志们整修房子的工人吃饭。我从旅馆那件事上得到的教训是：不先安顿实际干活的人，就要损失大量宝贵时间。佐林同意了，答应几天内交给我们办。可是一个星期过去，这件号称「急件」的事再无下文。过了一阵，佐林来电约我们同去岛上。到那儿一看，六七个政委已经接管了，几十个人在旁边闲荡。

===Original===

Zorin reassured us that matters would arrange themselves and that we should have an opportunity to organize the work as planned. However, we soon realized that the newly fledged officialdom was as hard to cope with as the old bureaucracy.
Every Commissar had his favourites whom he managed to list as employed on the job, thereby entitling them to bread rations and a meal. Thus almost before any actual workers appeared on the scene, eighty alleged “technicians” were already in possession of dinner tickets and bread cards. The men actually mobilized for the work received hardly anything. The result was general sabotage. Most of the men sent over to prepare the rest homes for the workers came from concentration camps: they were convicts and military deserters. I had often watched them at work, and in justice to them it must be said that they did not overexert themselves. “Why should we,” they would say; “we are fed on Sovietski soup; dirty dishwater it is, and we receive only what is left over from the idlers who order us about. And who will rest in these homes? Not we or our brothers in the factories. Only those who belong to the party or who have a pull will enjoy this place. Besides, the spring is near; we are needed at home on the farm. Why are we kept here?” Indeed, they did not exert themselves, those stalwart sons of Russia’s soil. There was no incentive: they had no point of contact with the life about them, and there was no one who could translate to them the meaning of work in revolutionary Russia. They were dazed by war, revolution, and hunger﻿—nothing could rouse them out of their stupor.
Many of the buildings on Kameniy Ostrov had been taken up for boarding schools and homes for defectives; some were occupied by old professors, teachers, and other intellectuals. Since the Revolution these people lived there unmolested, but now orders came to vacate, to make room for the rest homes. As almost no provision had been made to supply the dispossessed ones with other quarters, they were practically forced into the streets. Those friendly with Zinoviev, Gorky, or other influential Communists took their troubles to them, but persons lacking “pull” found no redress. The scenes of misery which I was compelled to witness daily exhausted my energies. It was all unnecessarily cruel, impractical, without any bearing on the Revolution.

===Chinese===

佐林安慰我们说，事情自会理顺，我们会有机会按计划组织工作。然而我们很快就明白：这批新出炉的官班子，同旧官僚一样难缠。

每个政委都有自己的亲信，都设法把亲信列上在编名单，好领面包配给和一顿饭。于是，真正的工人还没露面，八十个所谓的「技术员」已经揣上了饭票和面包卡，而真正调来干活的人几乎什么也领不到。结果是普遍怠工。派来整修工人休养之家的人，多数来自集中营：刑事犯和军事逃兵。我常看他们干活，平心而论，他们确实不肯多出一分力。「凭什么卖力？」他们说，「我们喝的是苏维埃汤——脏洗碗水；领的净是那帮指挥我们的人吃剩的。这些休养之家谁来住？不是我们，也不是厂里的弟兄。只有党里的人、有门路的人才能享这个福。再说开春了，家里地里等着人，凭什么扣我们在这儿？」确实，这些俄国土地的壮实儿子们不肯出力。没有任何动力：他们与周围的生活没有一丝牵连，也没有人能把革命俄国里劳动的意义讲给他们听。战争、革命、饥饿把他们打懵了——什么也无法把他们从麻木中唤起。

卡缅内岛上许多楼已被寄宿学校和缺陷人收容所占用，有些住着老教授、教师和其他知识分子。革命以来，这些人一直相安无事住在那里，如今却来了腾房的命令，要给休养之家让地方。被赶出的人几乎没有另行安置，等于被逼上街头。有面子结识季诺维也夫、高尔基或其他有权势共产党人的，还能把苦楚递上去；没有「门路」的，投诉无门。我天天被迫目睹的惨状耗尽了我的精力。这一切都残酷得毫无必要，全无实际意义，与革命毫不相干。

===Original===

Added to this was the chaos and confusion which prevailed. The bureaucratic officials seemed to take particular delight in countermanding each other’s orders. Houses already in the process of renovation, and on which much work and material were spent, would suddenly be left unfinished and some other work begun. Mansions filled with art treasures were turned into night lodgings, and dirty iron cots put among antique furniture and oil paintings﻿—an incongruous, stupid waste of time and energy. Zorin would frequently hold consultations by the hour with the staff of artists and engineers making plans for theatres, lecture halls, and amusement places, while the Commissars sabotaged the work. I stood the painful and ridiculous situation for two weeks, then gave up the matter in despair.
Early in May the workers’ rest homes on Kameniy Ostrov were opened with much pomp, music, and speeches. Glowing accounts were sent broadcast of the marvellous things done for the workers in Russia. In reality, it was Coney Island transferred to the environs of Petrograd, a gaudy showplace for credulous visitors. From that time on Zorin’s demeanour to me changed. He became cold, even antagonistic. No doubt he began to sense the struggle which was going on within me, and the break which was bound to come. I did, however, see much of Lisa Zorin, who had just become a mother. I nursed her and her baby, glad of the opportunity thus to express my gratitude for the warm friendship the Zorins had shown me during my first months in Russia. I appreciated their sterling honesty and devotion. Both were so favourably placed politically that they could be supplied with everything they wanted, yet Lisa Zorin lacked the simplest garments for her baby. “Thousands of Russian working women have no more, and why should I?” Lisa would say. When she was so weak that she could not nurse her baby, Zorin could not be induced to ask for special rations. I had to conspire against them by buying eggs and butter on the market to save the lives of mother and child. But their fine quality of character made my inner struggle the more difficult. Reason urged me to look the social facts in the face. My personal attachment to the Communists I had learned to know and esteem refused to accept the facts.

===Chinese===

再加上无处不在的乱成一团。官僚们似乎以互相推翻对方的命令为乐：有的房子整修到一半，投了大量工料，忽然撂下，另起一摊；藏满艺术珍品的宅邸改成了夜宿处，脏铁床架摆到古董家具和油画中间——荒唐而愚蠢的时间与精力的浪费。佐林时常同艺术家和工程师们一连几个钟头开会，规划剧场、讲堂、游乐场，政委们却在底下怠工。这场又痛心又荒唐的戏我撑了两个星期，绝望地撒手了。

五月初，卡缅内岛的工人休养之家在鼓乐与演说中隆重开放。广播里发出了火热的报导，说俄国为工人创造了何等的奇迹。实际上，那是搬到彼得格勒郊外的科尼岛，一个给轻信的参观者看的俗艳展览。从那时起，佐林对我的态度变了，冷淡，甚至带敌意。无疑，他开始觉察到我内心的斗争，觉察到那必将到来的决裂。但我仍常见莉莎·佐林——她刚做了母亲。我照料她和婴儿，很高兴能借此报答佐林夫妇我抵俄头几个月的 warm 情谊。我器重他们纯正的诚实与献身精神。两人政治上地位那样有利，要什么有什么，可莉莎·佐林连婴儿最简单的衣裳都缺。「成千上万俄国女工也不过如此，我凭什么特殊？」莉莎说。她虚弱得奶不动孩子了，佐林还是不肯去申请特别口粮。我只好背着他俩到市场上买鸡蛋黄油，救母子俩的命。但他们品格这样高尚，反而使我内心的挣扎更苦。理智敦促我正视社会现实，我对相识敬重的共产党人的私人情谊却拒绝接受现实。

===Original===

Never mind the evils﻿—I would say to myself﻿—as long as there are such as the Zorins and the Balabanovas, there must be something vital in the ideas they represent. I held on tenaciously to the phantom I had myself created.

===Chinese===

别管那些弊害——我对自己说——只要有佐林夫妇、巴拉班诺娃这样的人在，他们所代表的观念里必有活的东西。我死死抓着那个我自己造出来的幻影。

## XXIII Returning to Moscow / 二十三、返回莫斯科
===Original===

In a country where speech and press are so completely suppressed as in Russia it is not surprising that the human mind should feed on fancy and out of it weave the most incredible stories. Already, during my first months in Petrograd, I was amazed at the wild rumours that circulated in the city and were believed even by intelligent people. The Soviet press was inaccessible to the population at large and there was no other news medium. Every morning Bolshevik bulletins and papers were pasted on the street corners, but in the bitter cold few people cared to pause to read them. Besides, there was little faith in the Communist press. Petrograd was therefore completely cut off, not only from the Western world but even from the rest of Russia. An old revolutionist once said to me: “We not only don’t know what is going on in the world or in Moscow; we are not even aware of what is happening in the next street.” However, the human mind will not be bottled up all the time. It must have and generally finds an outlet. Rumours of attempted raids on Petrograd, stories that Zinoviev had been ducked in “Sovietsky soup” by some factory workers and that Moscow was captured by the Whites were afloat.
Of Odessa it was related that enemy ships had been sighted off the coast, and there was much talk of an impending attack. Yet when we arrived we found the city quiet and leading its ordinary life. Except for the large markets, Odessa impressed me as a complete picture of Soviet rule. But we had not been gone a day from the city when, on our return to Moscow, we again met the same rumours. The success of the Polish forces and the hasty retreat of the Red Army furnished fuel to the overexcited imagination of the people. Everywhere the roads were blocked with military trains and the stations filled with soldiers spreading the panic of the rout.
At several points the Soviet authorities were getting ready to evacuate at the first approach of danger. The population, however, could not do that. At the railroad stations along the route groups of people stood about discussing the impending attack. Fighting in Rostov, other cities already in the hands of Wrangel, bandits holding up trains and blowing up bridges, and similar stories kept everybody in a panic. It was of course impossible to verify the rumours.

===Chinese===

在一个言论与出版被压制得像俄国这样彻底的国度，人心只能靠幻想滋养，并从幻想里编出最离奇的故事，这不奇怪。早在彼得格勒的头几个月，城里流传的那些荒唐谣言就已令我惊叹，连聪明人也信。苏维埃的报刊大众看不到，又没有别的消息渠道。每天清晨，布尔什维克的公报和报纸贴在街角，可天寒地冻，少有人停步去读；何况人们对共产党报刊本就没信心。于是彼得格勒完全与世隔绝——不仅与西方世界隔绝，也与俄国其他地方隔绝。一位老革命家对我说过：「我们不但不知道世界和莫斯科的情形，连隔壁街上发生了什么都不知道。」然而人心不可能永远塞着，它必须有出口，而且总能找到。于是什么谣传都有：有人要偷袭彼得格勒；季诺维也夫被某厂工人按到「苏维埃汤」里浸了；莫斯科被白军占了。

关于敖德萨，说沿海发现了敌舰，进攻迫在眉睫。可我们到时，城里安安静静，一切照常。除了那几个大集市，敖德萨给我的印象是苏维埃统治的一幅完整图景。可我们离开那城不到一天，回莫斯科的路上，同样的谣言又迎面而来。波兰军队得手、红军仓皇撤退的消息，给人们过度兴奋的想象力添了柴。路上处处军列堵塞，车站满是散布溃败恐慌的士兵。

有几处，苏维埃当局已在准备一有危险迹象就撤。老百姓却走不了。沿途各站，人群三五成群议论着即将到来的进攻：罗斯托夫在打仗；别的城已落入弗兰格尔之手；土匪劫火车、炸桥梁——种种传闻闹得人人自危。谣言当然无从核实。

===Original===

But we were informed that we could not continue to Rostov-on-the-Don, that city being already within the military zone. We were advised to start for Kiev and thence return to Moscow. It was hard to give up our plan of reaching Baku, but we had no choice. We could not venture too far, especially as our car permit was to expire within a short time. We decided to return to Moscow via Kiev.
When we left Petrograd, we had promised to bring back from the South some sugar, white flour, and cereals for our starved friends who had lacked these necessities for three years. On the way to Kiev and Odessa we found provisions comparatively cheap; but now the prices had risen several hundred percent. From an Odessa friend we learned of a place twenty versts20 from Rakhno, a small village near Zhmerenka, where sugar, honey, and apple jelly could be had at small cost. We were not supposed to transport provisions to Petrograd, though our car was immune from the usual inspection by the Cheka. But as we had no intention of selling anything, we felt justified in bringing some food for people who had been starving for years. We had our car detached at Zhmerenka, and two men of the expedition and myself went to Rakhno.
It was no easy matter to induce the Zhmerenka peasants to take us to the next village. Would we give them salt, nails, or some other merchandise? Otherwise they would not go. We lost the best part of a day in a vain search, but at last we found a man who consented to drive us to the place in return for Kerensky rubles. The journey reminded me of the rocky road of good intentions: we were heaved up and down, jerked back and forth, like so many dice. After a seemingly endless trip, aching in every limb, we reached the village. It was poor and squalid, Jews constituting the main population. The peasants lived along the Rakhno road and visited the place only on market days. The Soviet officials were Gentiles.
We carried a letter of introduction to a woman physician, the sister of our Odessa Bundist friend. She was to direct us how to go about procuring the provisions. Arriving at the Doctor’s house we found her living in two small rooms, ill kept and unclean, with a dirty baby crawling about. The woman was busy making apple jelly. She was of the type of disillusioned intellectual now so frequently met in Russia.

===Chinese===

但我们被告知，顿河罗斯托夫去不成了，那城已划入军事区。有人建议我们先到基辅，再从那里回莫斯科。放弃去巴库的计划很难受，可没有选择：不能冒进太深，何况车厢通行证马上到期。我们决定取道基辅返回莫斯科。

离开彼得格勒时，我们答应过给挨饿的朋友们从南方带些糖、白面和粮食——他们缺这些东西已经三年。赴基辅和敖德萨的路上，食物相对便宜；可如今物价涨了好几倍。一位敖德萨朋友告诉我们，离拉赫诺二十俄里20 的一个地方——拉赫诺是兹梅林卡附近的小村——能便宜买到糖、蜂蜜和苹果酱。按规定我们不能往彼得格勒运粮，虽然我们的车厢免受契卡的例行检查。但我们并不打算出售任何东西，为饿了多年的人带点吃的，我们自觉理直气壮。我们在兹梅林卡摘下车厢，考察团两名成员和我一起去了拉赫诺。

要说服兹梅林卡的农民送我们去邻村，可不是容易事。给他们盐、钉子或其他什么货吗？不然人家不去。白白找了大半天，总算有一个同意收克伦斯基卢布送我们去。这一路使我想起「良好愿望的石头路」：我们像几粒骰子，被颠上颠下、甩前甩后。仿佛无穷无尽的车程之后，我们浑身酸痛地到了那村子。村子穷酸肮脏，居民以犹太人为主。农民们沿拉赫诺大道而居，只有集市日才进城。苏维埃官员都是非犹太人。

我们带着写给一位女医生的介绍信——我们那位敖德萨崩得朋友的妹妹，由她指点我们如何采买。到她家一看，她住在两间小屋里，屋子疏于收拾、不干净，一个脏娃娃在爬。女医生正忙着熬苹果酱。她属于那种幻灭了的知识分子——如今俄国随处可见。

===Original===

From her conversation I learned that she and her husband, also a physician, had been detailed to that desolate spot. They were completely isolated from all intellectual life, having neither papers, books, nor associates. Her husband would begin his rounds early in the morning and return late at night, while she had to attend to her baby and household, besides taking care of her own patients. She had only recently recovered from typhus and it was hard for her to chop wood, carry water, wash and cook and look after her sick. But what made their life unbearable was the general antagonism to the intelligentsia. They had it constantly thrown up to them that they were bourgeois and counterrevolutionists, and they were charged with sabotage. It was only for the sake of her child that she continued the sordid life, the woman said; “otherwise it were better to be dead.”
A young woman, poorly clad, but clean and neat, came to the house and was introduced as a school teacher. She at once got into conversation with me. She was a Communist, she announced, who was “doing her own thinking.” “Moscow may be autocratic,” she said, “but the authorities in the towns and villages here beat Moscow. They do as they please.” The provincial officials were flotsam washed ashore by the great storm. They had no revolutionary past﻿—they had known no suffering for their ideals. They were just slaves in positions of power. If she had not been a Communist herself, she would have been eliminated long ago, but she was determined to make a fight against the abuses in her district. As to the schools, they were doing as best they could under the circumstances, but that was very little. They lacked everything. It was not so bad in the summer, but in the winter the children had to stay home because the class rooms were not heated. Was it true that Moscow was publishing glowing accounts of the great reduction in illiteracy? Well, it was certainly exaggerated. In her village the progress was very slow. She had often wondered whether there was really much to so-called education. Supposing the peasants should learn to read and write. Would that make them better and kinder men? If so, why is there so much cruelty, injustice, and strife in countries where people are not illiterate? The Russian peasant cannot read or write, but he has an innate sense of right and beauty.

===Chinese===

从谈话中我得知，她和她丈夫——也是医生——被派到这个荒凉的地方。他们同一切智识生活隔绝：没有报纸，没有书，没有同道。丈夫一清早出诊，深夜方归；她要看孩子、操持家务，还得照料自己的病人。她伤寒初愈，劈柴挑水、洗涮做饭、照看病患，样样艰难。可最叫他们受不了的，是对知识分子的普遍敌意：资产阶级、反革命的帽子时时扣过来，还常被指为怠工。她说，只是为了孩子，她才续着这龌龊的日子，「不然还不如死了干净。」

一个年轻女子来到屋里，衣着寒素却干净整齐，介绍说是小学教师。她立刻同我攀谈起来。她宣布自己是共产党人，但「自己动脑子」。「莫斯科也许专制，」她说，「可这儿的城乡当局比莫斯科还厉害，为所欲为。」外省官员是大风暴冲上岸的浮渣：没有革命经历，没为理想吃过苦，不过是坐在权力位子上的奴隶罢了。她说，若不是她自己也是共产党人，早被清洗掉了；但她决意同本区的弊政斗一斗。至于学校，情形所限，尽力而为，可所能为的很少，什么都缺。夏天还好，冬天教室没火，孩子们只能待在家里。莫斯科不是在大肆宣扬扫盲成绩斐然吗？唉，实在夸大了：她那个村进展很慢。她常常怀疑，所谓教育究竟有多大意思。就算农民学会了读写，人就变得更好、更善良了吗？若是，为什么在不识字者寥寥的国家里，残忍、不义与倾轧照样那么多？俄国农民不识字，可他天生懂得正与美。

===Original===

He can do wonderful things with his hands and he is no more brutal than the rest of the world.
I was interested to find such an unusual viewpoint in one so young and in such an out-of-the-way place. The little teacher could not have been more than twenty-five. I encouraged her to speak of her reactions to the general policies and methods of her party. Did she approve of them, did she think them dictated by the revolutionary process? She was not a politician, she said; she did not know. She could judge only by the results and they were far from satisfactory. But she had faith in the Revolution. It had uprooted the very soil, it had given life a new meaning. Even the peasants were not the same﻿—no one was the same. Something great must come of all the confusion.
The arrival of the Doctor turned the conversation into other channels. When informed of our errand he went in search of some tradesmen, but presently he returned to say that nothing could be done: it was the eve of Yom Kippur, and every Jew was in the synagogue. Heathen that I am, I did not know that I had come on the eve of that most solemn fast day. As we could not remain another day, we decided to return without having accomplished our purpose.
Here a new difficulty arose. Our driver would not budge unless we got an armed guard to accompany us. He was afraid of bandits: two nights previously, he said, they had attacked travellers in the forest. It became necessary to apply to the Chairman of the Militia. The latter was willing to help us, but all his men were in the synagogue, praying. Would we wait until the services were over?
At last the people filed out from the synagogue and we were given two armed militiamen. It was rather hard on those Jewish boys, for it was a sin to ride on Yom Kippur. But no inducement could persuade the peasant to venture through the woods without military protection. Life is indeed a crazy quilt made of patches. The peasant, a true Ukrainian, would not have hesitated a moment to beat and rob Jews in a pogrom; yet he felt secure in the protection of Jews against the possible attack of his own coreligionists.
We rode into the bright fall night, the sky dotted with stars. It was soothingly still, with all nature asleep. The driver and our escort discussed the bandits, competing in bloodcurdling stories of the outrages committed by them.

===Chinese===

他的一双手能创造奇迹，也并不比世界别处的人更粗野。

在这样年轻的人身上、这样偏僻的地方，听到这样不同寻常的见解，令我感兴趣。小教师顶多不过二十五岁。我鼓励她谈谈对本党总政策、总方针的反应：她赞成吗？认为那是革命进程使然吗？她说她不是政治家，不懂；她只能看结果，而结果远不能令人满意。但她对革命有信心：革命翻了天，给了生命新的意义，连农民都变了——人人都不一样了。这么大的动荡，总会生出些伟大的东西。

医生回来了，话头转到别处。得知我们的来意，他去找商贩，却很快回来说：一点办法也没有，这天是赎罪日前夜，犹太人都进了会堂。我这个异教徒，竟不知道自己撞上了这个最庄严的斋日前夜。第二天走不了，我们决定无功而返。

新的难题又来了：车夫不肯动身，除非我们弄到武装卫兵护送。他怕土匪：他说，就在两夜前，林子里有行路人遭了劫。只好去求民兵主席。主席愿意帮忙，可他的人都在会堂里祈祷。要等礼拜结束吗？

人们终于鱼贯走出会堂，给我们派了两名武装民兵。这可苦了那两个犹太小伙子——赎罪日乘车是罪过。但任凭什么也劝不动那农民无保护闯林子。生活真是块百衲的布：这农民，十足的乌克兰人，若是碰上排犹暴乱，打劫犹太人他不会犹豫一秒；可现在，为防备自己同教伙伴可能的袭击，他却觉得有犹太卫兵护着才安全。

我们驶入明亮的秋夜，满天星斗。四野悄静，万物安睡。车夫和卫兵聊着土匪，一个比一个讲得毛骨悚然。

===Original===

As we reached the dark forest I reflected that their loud voices would be the signal of our approach for any highwaymen who might be lying in wait. The soldiers stood up in the wagon, their rifles ready for action; the peasant crossed himself and lashed the horses into a mad gallop, keeping up the pace till we reached the open road again. It was all very exciting but we met no bandits. They must have been sabotaging that night.
We reached the station too late to make connections and had to wait until the morning. I spent the night in the company of a girl in soldier uniform, a Communist. She had been at every front, she declared, and had fought many bandits. She was a sort of Playboy of the Eastern World, romancing by the hour. Her favourite stories were of shooting. “A bunch of counterrevolutionists, White Guards and speculators,” she would say; “they should all be shot.” I thought of the little school teacher, the lovely spirit in the village, giving of herself in hard and painful service to the children, to beauty in life; and here, her comrade, also a young woman, but hardened and cruel, lacking all sense of revolutionary values﻿—both children of the same school, yet so unlike each other.
In the morning we rejoined the Expedition in Zhmerenka and proceeded to Kiev, where we arrived by the end of September, to find the city completely changed. The panic of the Twelfth Army was in the air; the enemy was supposed to be only 150 versts21 away and many Soviet Departments were being evacuated, adding to the general uneasiness and fright. I visited Wetoshkin, the Chairman of the Revkom, and his secretary. The latter inquired about Odessa, anxious to know how they were doing there, whether they had suppressed trade, and how the Soviet Departments were working. I told him of the general sabotage, of the speculation and the horrors of the Cheka. As to trade, the stores were closed and all signs were down, but the markets were doing big business. “Indeed? Well, you must tell this to Comrade Wetoshkin,” the Secretary cried gleefully. “What do you suppose﻿—Rakovsky was here and told us perfect wonders about the accomplishments of Odessa. He put us on the rack because we had not done as much. You must tell Wetoshkin all about Odessa; he will enjoy the joke on Rakovsky.”
I met Wetoshkin on the stairs as I was leaving the office.

===Chinese===

驶近黑黢黢的森林时，我心想：真有强盗埋伏，他们这么大的嗓门正好通报我们来了。士兵在车斗里站起身，端枪待发；农民画了个十字，狠抽马匹狂奔，一口气跑到开阔的大路才缓下来。这一路够刺激，可我们没遇上土匪——他们那晚想必也在怠工。

到车站太晚，接不上车，只好等天亮。那一夜陪我同屋的是个穿军装的姑娘，共产党人。她说她打遍了各条战线，毙过多少土匪。她简直是「东部世界的风流儿」，一口气编几个钟头的传奇。她最爱讲枪毙人。「一伙反革命、白卫军和投机客，」她说，「统统该枪毙。」我想起村里那位小教师，那个可爱的灵魂，在艰苦而痛苦的岗位上把自己献给孩子们、献给生活中的美；而这里，她的同志，同样年轻的女人，却已冷酷硬化，毫无革命价值的意识——同一所学校的孩子，彼此天差地别。

早晨，我们在兹梅林卡与考察团会合，前往基辅，九月底抵达。城里全变了样：第十二军溃败的恐慌弥漫在空气里，据说敌人只有一百五十俄里21 之遥，许多苏维埃机关正在疏散，更添了普遍的不安与惊惶。我去见革委会主席韦托什金和他的秘书。秘书打听敖德萨，急于知道那边干得怎么样：取缔贸易没有？各机关运转如何？我讲了普遍的怠工、投机和契卡的恐怖。至于贸易——铺子是关了，招牌也摘了，可集市生意兴隆。「真的吗？那您一定得告诉韦托什金同志，」秘书喜形于色，「您猜怎么着——拉柯夫斯基来过，把敖德萨的成就夸成了一朵花，还数落我们没人家干得好。您务必把敖德萨的事全告诉韦托什金，他准会拿拉柯夫斯基的笑话开心。」

我离开办公室时，在楼梯上碰见了韦托什金。

===Original===

He looked thinner than when I had last seen him, and very worried. When asked about the impending danger, he made light of it. “We are not going to evacuate,” he said, “we remain right here. It is the only way to reassure the public.” He, too, inquired about Odessa. I promised to call again later, as, I had no time just then, but I did not have the chance to see Wetoshkin again to furnish that joke on Rakovsky. We left Kiev within two days.
At Bryansk, an industrial centre not far away from Moscow, we came upon large posters announcing that Makhno was again with the Bolsheviki, and that he was distinguishing himself by daring exploits against Wrangel. It was startling news, in view of the fact that the Soviet papers had constantly painted Makhno as a bandit, counterrevolutionary, and traitor. What had happened to bring about this change of attitude and tone? The thrilling adventure of having our car held up and ourselves carried off as prisoners by the Makhnovtsi did not come off. By the time we reached the district where Makhno had been operating in September, he was cut off from us. It would have been very interesting to meet the peasant leader face to face and hear at first hand what he was about. He was undoubtedly the most picturesque and vital figure brought to the fore by the Revolution in the South﻿—and now he was again with the Bolsheviki. What had happened? There was no way of knowing until we should reach Moscow.
From a copy of the *Izvestia* that fell into our hands en route, we learned the sad news of the death of John Reed. It was a great blow to those of us who had known Jack. The last time I saw him was at the guest house, the Hôtel International, in Petrograd. He had just returned from Finland, after his imprisonment there, and was ill in bed. I was informed that Jack was alone and without proper care, and I went up to nurse him. He was in a bad state, all swollen and with a nasty rash on his arms, the result of malnutrition. In Finland he had been fed almost exclusively on dried fish and had been otherwise wretchedly treated. He was a very sick man, but his spirit remained the same. No matter how radically one disagreed with Jack, one could not help loving his big, generous spirit, and now he was dead, his life laid down in the service of the Revolution, as he believed.

===Chinese===

他比上次见面瘦了，满面忧色。问起迫在眉睫的危险，他不以为意：「我们不疏散，就在这儿待着。这是安民心的唯一办法。」他也问起敖德萨。我答应稍后再来讲——当时没有时间，可后来再没机会见到韦托什金，没能把拉柯夫斯基那个笑话讲给他听。两天之内我们就离开了基辅。

在离莫斯科不远的工业中心布良斯克，我们看见大幅招贴，宣布马赫诺重又同布尔什维克合作，正以果敢的战绩打击弗兰格尔。这消息令人震惊：要知道，苏维埃报纸一直把马赫诺画成强盗、反革命、叛徒。是什么促成了这态度与腔调的转变？我们被马赫诺派截车、掳为人质的那场奇遇，终究没有发生。我们抵达马赫诺九月活动的地区时，他已同我们隔开了。同这位农民领袖面对面、听他亲口讲讲他在干什么，本会极有意思——他无疑是南方革命推到前台的最有声色、最有生命力的人物，如今却又同布尔什维克站到了一起。发生了什么？到了莫斯科才能知道。

途中我们弄到一份《消息报》，得知约翰·里德去世的噩耗。对认识杰克的人来说，这是沉重的打击。我最后一次见到他，是在彼得格勒的宾馆国际旅馆。他刚结束芬兰的监禁回来，卧病在床。听说杰克孤身一人无人照应，我便去护理他。他情形很糟：浑身浮肿，臂上起着凶险的皮疹——营养不良的结果。在芬兰，他几乎只靠鱼干果腹，还受了种种虐待。他病得很重，精神却依旧。无论你同杰克的分歧多大，你没法不爱他那颗宽厚慷慨的心。如今他死了，把生命交给了他信仰中的革命事业。

===Original===

Arriving in Moscow I immediately went to the guest house, the Delovoi Dvor, where stayed Louise Bryant, Jack’s wife. I found her terribly distraught and glad to see one who had known Jack so well. We talked of him, of his illness, his suffering and his untimely death. She was much embittered because, she claimed, Jack had been ordered to Baku to attend the Congress of the Eastern peoples when he was already very ill. He returned a dying man. But even then he could have been saved had he been given competent medical attention. He lay in his room for a week without the doctors making up their mind as to the nature of his illness. Then it was too late. I could well understand Louise’s feelings, though I was convinced that everything humanly possible had been done for Reed. I knew that whatever else might be said against the Bolsheviki, it could not be charged that they neglect those who serve them. On the contrary, they are generous masters. But Louise had lost what was most precious to her.
During the conversation she asked me about my experiences and I told her of the conflict within me, of the desperate effort I had been making to find my way out of the chaos, and that now the fog was lifting, and I was beginning to differentiate between the Bolsheviki and the Revolution. Ever since I had come to Russia I had begun to sense that all was not well with the Bolshevik regime, and I felt as if caught in a trap. “How uncanny!” Louise suddenly gripped my arm and stared at me with wild eyes. “ ‘Caught in a trap’ were the very words Jack repeated in his delirium.” I realized that poor Jack had also begun to see beneath the surface. His was the free, unfettered spirit striving for the real values of life. It would be chafed when bound by a dogma which proclaimed itself immutable. Had Jack lived he would no doubt have clung valiantly to the thing which had caught him in the trap. But in the face of death the mind of man sometimes becomes luminous: it sees in a flash what in man’s normal condition is obscure and hidden from him. It was not at all strange to me that Jack should have felt as I did, as everyone who is not a zealot must feel in Russia﻿—caught in a trap.

===Chinese===

一到莫斯科，我就直奔宾馆德沃洛伊大院，杰克的妻子路易丝·布赖恩特住在那里。见她心烦意乱到极点，见到我这个深知杰克的人也觉宽慰。我们谈他，谈他的病、他的苦和他不逢时的死。她满腔怨愤，说杰克已经病得很重，还被派去巴库出席东方各民族大会，回来已是个垂死之人；而即便那时，若得妥善医治，本还可以救——他在房间里躺了一个星期，医生们还没拿准他得的是什么病，然后一切都晚了。路易丝的心情我完全理解，虽然我确信，人所能为里德做的一切都已做了。我知道，布尔什维克再怎么样也可指摘不到「怠慢效命者」这一条：相反，他们是大方的主子。可路易丝失去了她最珍贵的东西。

谈话间她问起我的经历，我对她讲了内心的冲突，讲我如何拼力想从混沌中找出路，讲如今迷雾渐散，我开始分清布尔什维克与革命。自抵俄以来，我渐渐察觉布尔什维克政权并非一切安好，觉得自己像落进了陷阱。「多玄啊！」路易丝突然攥住我的胳膊，瞪大了眼睛瞪着我。「『落进了陷阱』——杰克昏迷时翻来覆去说的，正是这几个字。」我明白了：可怜的杰克也开始看到表层之下。他那自由不羁的灵魂追求着生活真正的价值，被一套自称不可易移的教义捆住时，必然被磨得生疼。倘若活着，杰克无疑仍会英勇地抱着那困住他的东西不放。但面对死亡，人的心智有时会豁然明亮：平常遮蔽着的东西，一瞬之间看清了。杰克临终的感受同我一样——同俄国每一个非狂热者必有的感受一样：落进了陷阱——这在我看来毫不奇怪。

## X The British Labour Mission / 十、英国劳工代表团
===Original===

I was glad to learn that Angelica Balabanova arrived in Petrograd to prepare quarters for the British Labour Mission. During my stay in Moscow I had come to know and appreciate the fine spirit of Angelica. She was very devoted to me and when I fell ill she gave much time to my care, procured medicine which could be obtained only in the Kremlin drug store, and got special sick rations for me. Her friendship was generous and touching, and she endeared herself very much to me.
The Narishkin Palace was to be prepared for the Mission, and Angelica invited me to accompany her there. I noticed that she looked more worn and distressed than when I had seen her in Moscow. Our conversation made it clear to me that she suffered keenly from the reality which was so unlike her ideal. But she insisted that what seemed failure to me was conditioned in life itself, itself the greatest failure.
Narishkin Palace is situated on the southern bank of the Neva, almost opposite the Peter-and-Paul Fortress. The place was prepared for the expected guests and a number of servants and cooks installed to minister to their needs. Soon the Mission arrived﻿—most of them typical workingmen delegates﻿—and with them a staff of newspaper men and Mrs. Snowden. The most outstanding figure among them was Bertrand Russell, who quickly demonstrated his independence and determination to be free to investigate and learn at first hand.
In honour of the Mission the Bolsheviki organized a great demonstration on the Uritski Square. Thousands of people, among them women and children, came to show their gratitude to the English labour representatives for venturing into revolutionary Russia. The ceremony consisted of the singing of the “Internationale,” followed by music and speeches, the latter translated by Balabanova in masterly fashion. Then came the military exercises. I heard Mrs. Snowden say disapprovingly, “What a display of military!” I could not resist the temptation of remarking: “Madame, remember that the big Russian army is largely the making of your own country. Had England not helped to finance the invasions into Russia, the latter could put its soldiers to useful labour.”
The British Mission was entertained royally with theatres, operas, ballets, and excursions. Luxury was heaped upon them while the people slaved and went hungry.

===Chinese===

得知安热莉卡·巴拉班诺娃到了彼得格勒，为英国工党代表团打点住处，我很高兴。在莫斯科期间，我已了解并敬重安热莉卡的高尚品性。她待我极尽关爱：我病倒时，她花许多时间照料，弄到只有克里姆林宫药房才有的药，还为我申请了特别的病号口粮。她的友情慷慨而感人，令我深深依恋。

纳雷什金宫被定为代表团下榻处，安热莉卡邀我同去。我注意到她比在莫斯科见时更憔悴、更愁苦。交谈使我明白：与理想如此悬殊的现实，令她痛彻。但她坚持说，在我看来是失败的东西，是生活本身使然——生活本身原是最大的失败。

纳雷什金宫坐落在涅瓦河南岸，几乎正对彼得保罗要塞。为贵客们备好了住处，还安置了一班仆役厨子伺候。代表团很快到了——多为典型的工人代表——随行还有一批报人和斯诺登夫人。其中最抢眼的人物是伯特兰·罗素，他很快显出独立不羁的品性，坚持要自由考察、亲获一手材料。

为欢迎代表团，布尔什维克在乌里茨基广场组织了盛大示威。成千上万的人，其中有妇女儿童，前来向敢来革命俄国的英国劳工代表致谢。仪式是唱《国际歌》，随后音乐与演说，演说由巴拉班诺娃出色地翻译。然后是军事操演。我听见斯诺登夫人不以为然地说：「好一场耀武扬威！」我忍不住接了一句：「夫人，请别忘了，俄国这支庞大的军队多半是贵国的手笔。若不是英国出资资助对俄国的入侵，俄国早把它的兵派去干有用的活了。」

英国代表团受到王侯般的款待：剧院、歌剧、芭蕾、游览，一应俱全。奢侈堆在他们身上，而人民在苦役中挨饿。

===Original===

The Soviet Government left nothing undone to create a good impression and everything of a disturbing nature was kept from the visitors. Angelica hated the display and sham, and suffered keenly under the rigid watch placed upon every movement of the Mission. “Why should they not see the true state of Russia? Why should they not learn how the Russian people live?” she would lament. “Yet I am so impractical,” she would correct herself; “perhaps it is all necessary.” At the end of two weeks a farewell banquet was given to the visitors. Angelica insisted that I must attend. Again there were speeches and toasts, as is the custom at such functions. The speeches which seemed to ring most sincere were those of Balabanova and Madame Ravitch. The latter asked me to interpret her address, which I did. She spoke in behalf of the Russian women proletarians and praised their fortitude and devotion to the Revolution. “May the English proletarians learn the quality of their heroic Russian sisters,” concluded Madame Ravitch. Mrs. Snowden, the erstwhile suffragette, had not a word in reply. She preserved a “dignified” aloofness. However, the lady became enlivened when the speeches were over and she got busy collecting autographs.

===Chinese===

苏维埃政府为制造好印象无所不用其极，一切可能引起不快的东西都同客人隔绝。安热莉卡憎恶这种做戏与虚假，代表团一举一动都受严密监视，更使她深以为苦。「为什么不能让他们看看俄国的真实境况？为什么不能让他们知道俄国人民怎么生活？」她总这样叹。「可我又这么不切实际，」她又自我更正，「也许这一切都是必要的。」两周期满，为客人举办了告别宴会。安热莉卡坚持要我出席。照例是演说与祝酒。听来最真挚的，是巴拉班诺娃和拉维奇女史的演说。后者请我替她翻译致辞，我照办了。她代表俄国女无产者讲话，赞美她们对革命的坚忍与忠诚。「愿英国无产者学一学她们俄国姐妹的英雄品质。」拉维奇女史这样作结。斯诺登夫人——昔日的女权运动者——竟无一字回应，端着「矜持」的超然。不过演说一结束，这位夫人就活跃起来，忙着收集起签名来。

## VIII The First of May in Petrograd / 八、彼得格勒的五一节
===Original===

In 1890 the First of May was for the first time celebrated in America as Labour’s international holiday. May Day became to me a great, inspiring event. To witness the celebration of the First of May in a free country﻿—it was something to dream of, to long for, but perhaps never to be realized. And now, in 1920, the dream of many years was about to become real in revolutionary Russia. I could hardly await the morning of May First. It was a glorious day, with the warm sun melting away the last crust of the hard winter. Early in the morning strains of music greeted me: groups of workers and soldiers were marching through the streets, singing revolutionary songs. The city was gaily decorated: the Uritski Square, facing the Winter Palace, was a mass of red, the streets near by a veritable riot of colour. Great crowds were about, all wending their way to the Field of Mars where the heroes of the Revolution were buried.
Though I had an admission card to the reviewing stand I preferred to remain among the people, to feel myself a part of the great hosts that had brought about the world event. This was their day﻿—the day of their making. Yet﻿—they seemed peculiarly quiet, oppressively silent. There was no joy in their singing, no mirth in their laughter. Mechanically they marched, automatically they responded to the claqueurs on the reviewing stand shouting “Hurrah” as the columns passed.
In the evening a pageant was to take place. Long before the appointed hour the Uritski Square down to the palace and to the banks of the Neva was crowded with people gathered to witness the open-air performance symbolizing the triumph of the people. The play consisted of three parts, the first portraying the conditions which led up to the war and the role of the German Socialists in it; the second reproduced the February Revolution, with Kerensky in power; the last﻿—the October Revolution. It was a play beautifully set and powerfully acted, a play vivid, real, fascinating. It was given on the steps of the former Stock Exchange, facing the Square. On the highest step sat kings and queens with their courtiers, attended by soldiery in gay uniforms. The scene represents a gala court affair: the announcement is made that a monument is to be built in honour of world capitalism. There is much rejoicing, and a wild orgy of music and dance ensues.

===Chinese===

1890 年，五一节第一次在美国作为劳工的国际节日来庆祝。五一于我成了一件伟大而振奋的事。在自由的国度亲历五一庆典——那是可以梦想、可以渴望、却或许永难成真的事。而如今，1920 年，多年的梦就要在革命的俄国成真了。我几乎等不及五一的早晨。那是个晴朗的日子，暖阳融着苦冬最后一层硬壳。一大早，乐声便迎面而来：一队队工人和士兵唱着革命歌曲穿街而过。城市装点得喜气洋洋：冬宫对面的乌里茨基广场一片红海，近旁的街巷彩色斑爛，如闹如沸。人潮涌动，都往战神广场去——革命的英雄们安葬在那里。

我虽有观礼台的入场证，却宁愿留在人群中，让自己成为那造就了世界大事的伟大人群的一分子。这是他们的日子——他们亲手创造的日子。然而——他们显得异样地安静，静得压人。歌声里没有欢乐，笑里没有快意。他们机械地行进，机械地应和着观礼台上掌班的欢呼——队伍走过时，那里喊「乌拉」。

晚上要演露天戏。还早得很，从乌里茨基广场到冬宫、到涅瓦河岸，已是万头攒动，人们等着看那出象征人民凯旋的露天大戏。全剧分三幕：第一幕演大战的由来和德国社会党人在其中的角色；第二幕重现二月革命和克伦斯基当政；最后一幕——十月革命。戏排得漂亮，演得有力，鲜活、真实、攫人。舞台设在旧证券交易所的台阶上，面朝广场。最高的台阶上坐着国王和王后们，廷臣侍立，彩衣的士兵护卫。场面是盛大的宫廷庆典：宣布要建造一座纪念碑，献给世界资本主义。举座欢腾，音乐与舞蹈转入狂欢。

===Original===

Then from the depths there emerge the enslaved and toiling masses, their chains ringing mournfully to the music above. They are responding to the command to build the monument for their masters: some are seen carrying hammers and anvils; others stagger under the weight of huge blocks of stone and loads of brick. The workers are toiling in their world of misery and darkness, lashed to greater effort by the whip of the slave drivers, while above there is light and joy, and the masters are feasting. The completion of the monument is signalled by large yellow disks hoisted on high amidst the rejoicing of the world on top.
At this moment a little red flag is seen waving below, and a small figure is haranguing the people. Angry fists are raised and then flag and figure disappear, only to reappear again in different parts of the underworld. Again the red flag waves, now here, now there. The people slowly gain confidence and presently become threatening. Indignation and anger grow﻿—the kings and queens become alarmed. They fly to the safety of the citadels, and the army prepares to defend the stronghold of capitalism.
It is August, 1914. The rulers are again feasting, and the workers are slaving. The members of the Second International attend the confab of the mighty. They remain deaf to the plea of the workers to save them from the horrors of war. Then the strains of “God Save the King” announce the arrival of the English army. It is followed by Russian soldiers with machine guns and artillery, and a procession of nurses and cripples, the tribute to the Moloch of war.
The next act pictures the February Revolution. Red flags appear everywhere, armed motor cars dash about. The people storm the Winter Palace and haul down the emblem of Tsardom. The Kerensky Government assumes control, and the people are driven back to war. Then comes the marvellous scene of the October Revolution, with soldiers and sailors galloping along the open space before the white marble building. They dash up the steps into the palace, there is a brief struggle, and the victors are hailed by the masses in wild jubilation. The “Internationale” floats upon the air; it mounts higher and higher into exultant peals of joy. Russia is free﻿—the workers, sailors, and soldiers usher in the new era, the beginning of the world commune!
Tremendously stirring was the picture.

===Chinese===

这时，从底层深处走出了被奴役的劳苦大众，他们的锁链应和着上面的乐声，铿然作哀鸣。他们奉命为主子们造纪念碑：有的扛着铁锤铁砧，有的被巨石和成垛的砖压得踉跄。工人们在苦难与黑暗的世界里劳作，监工的鞭子抽打着他们拼命，而头顶上灯红酒绿，主子们在大宴。纪念碑落成的信号，是几面黄色的大圆盘高高升起，上层世界一片欢庆。

就在此刻，下方一面小红旗挥动起来，一个小小的身影正向人群演说。愤怒的拳头举起来了，随即旗帜与人影消失，又在底层各处重新出现。红旗一会儿在这儿、一会儿在那儿挥舞。人们渐渐壮起胆，继而现出威压之势。义愤与怒火增长——国王王后们惊慌起来，逃进堡垒，军队准备保卫资本主义的堡垒。

这是 1914 年 8 月。统治者照旧宴乐，工人照旧为奴。第二国际的成员们赴强权者的聚会，对工人救我们于战祸的哀求充耳不闻。接着，《天佑吾王》的旋律宣告英军登场，随后是携机枪火炮的俄国士兵，还有护士与残废者的行列——献给战争这尊摩洛的贡品。

下一幕是二月革命。红旗遍地，武装汽车飞驰。人民攻打冬宫，扯下沙皇制度的旗徽。克伦斯基政府接了权，人民又被赶回战场。然后是十月革命的奇景：士兵和水兵纵马奔驰在白色大理石宫殿前的空场上，冲上台阶杀进宫去，一场短促的搏斗，胜利者受到大众疯狂的欢呼致敬。《国际歌》响彻夜空，越升越高，化作欢腾的钟鸣。俄国自由了——工人、水兵、士兵迎来了新纪元，世界公社的开端！

这画面激动人心。

===Original===

But the vast mass remained silent. Only a faint applause was heard from the great throng. I was dumbfounded. How explain this astonishing lack of response? When I spoke to Lisa Zorin about it she said that the people had actually lived through the October Revolution, and that the performance necessarily fell flat by comparison with the reality of 1917. But my little Communist neighbour gave a different version. “The people had suffered so many disappointments since October, 1917,” she said, “that the Revolution has lost all meaning to them. The play had the effect of making their disappointment more poignant.”

===Chinese===

可是那茫茫的人群依然沉默，只从人海深处传来稀落的掌声。我懵住了：这惊人的没有反应，怎么解释？我对莉莎·佐林说起，她说，人民是亲身活过十月革命的，同 1917 年的真实相比，这出戏必然显得平淡。可我那位共产党小邻居另有说法：「十月以来人民受了太多失望，」她说，「革命在他们心里已经失去了一切意义。这出戏只使他们更加痛切地感到失望。」

## XXXII The Socialist Republic Resorts to Deportation / 三十二、社会主义共和国诉诸驱逐
===Original===

The Cheka had succeeded in terrorizing the whole people. The only exceptions were the politicals, whose courage and devotion to their ideals defied the Bolsheviki as it had the Romanovs. I knew many of those brave spirits, and I saw in them the only hope to sustain one amid the general wreckage. They were the living proof of the powerlessness of terror against an Ideal.
Typical of this class was a certain Anarchist who had long been sought for by the Cheka as an important Makhnovetz. He was a member of the military staff of the revolutionary *povstantsi* of the Ukraine and the close friend and counsellor of Makhno. He had already known him intimately when they were together in *katorga* in the days of the Tsar. He had shared all the hardships and danger of the *povstantsi* life and participated in their campaigns against the enemies of the Revolution. After the defeat of Wrangel and the last treachery of the Bolsheviki toward Makhno, when the latter’s army had become scattered and many of its members killed, this man succeeded in escaping the Bolshevik net. He determined to come to Moscow, there to write a history of Makhnovstchina. It was a perilous journey, made under most difficult conditions, with death constantly treading his footsteps. Under an assumed name he secured a tiny room in the environs of the capital. He lived in most abject poverty, always in danger of his life, visiting his wife in the city only under cover of darkness. Once in every twenty-four hours he would come to the appointed place for a little respite and his sole meal of the day, consisting of potatoes, herring, and tea. Every moment he risked being recognized, for he was well known in Moscow, and recognition meant summary execution. His wife also, if discovered, would have met the same fate﻿—the devoted woman who, though with child at the time, had followed him to Moscow. After a desperate hunt for employment she found a position in a crèche, but as pregnant women were not accepted in such institutions, she had to disguise her condition. All day long she had to be on her feet, attending to her duties, and living in constant fear for the safety of her husband.
When the baby was born the situation became more aggravated. The woman was harassed by her superiors because she had obtained the position without their knowledge of her condition.

===Chinese===

契卡已把全国吓服。唯一的例外是政治犯们——他们的勇气与对理想的忠诚，当年不惧罗曼诺夫，如今也不惧布尔什维克。这样的勇者我认得许多，在这普遍的残骸中间，我视他们为支撑一个人的唯一希望。他们是活的证明：恐怖在理想面前无能为力。

这一类人的典型，是一位契卡追捕已久的无政府主义者，罪名是马赫诺派要人。他是乌克兰革命*起义军*军事参谋部的成员，是马赫诺的挚友与谋主——早在沙皇时代，两人同在*苦役*中就相知甚深。他同*起义军*共过一切患难与危险，参加过他们打击革命敌人的历次战役。弗兰格尔败亡、布尔什维克对马赫诺使出最后一次背信之后——马赫诺的队伍已被打散，许多成员被杀——此人逃出了布尔什维克的天罗地网，决意去莫斯科，在那里写一部马赫诺运动史。那是一趟九死一生的旅程，条件极其恶劣，死神寸步不离。他化名在首都郊外弄到一间小屋，穷到极处，性命朝不保夕，只有在夜幕掩护下才进城看妻子。每二十四小时，他到约定的地方来一次，喘口气，吃一天里唯一的一顿饭：土豆、青鱼、茶。他时刻冒着被认出的危险——在莫斯科他很有名，被认出就意味着就地枪决。他妻子若被发现，也是同样的命运——这个忠贞的女人怀着身孕还跟他到了莫斯科。她拼命找到了一份托儿所的工作，可孕妇不在收用之列，只好遮掩身子，整天站着当班，又整天为丈夫的安危提心吊胆。

孩子降生后，处境更难了。上司因为她入职时隐瞒了身孕而处处刁难。

===Original===

Petty officialdom and hard work exhausted her energies and the daily anxiety about the man she loved nearly drove her frantic. Yet never a sign of all that troubled her when the man would visit her.
Many evenings I spent with this couple. They were entirely cut off from the outside world and former friends, all alone save for the fear of discovery and death which was their constant companion. In the dreary, damp room, the baby asleep, we passed many hours talking in subdued voices about the Ukrainian peasantry and the Makhno movement. My friend was familiar with every phase of it from personal experience, which he was now incorporating into his book on Makhno. He was absorbed in that work, which was for the first time to give to the world the truth about Makhno and the *povstantsi*. Deeply concerned about his wife and child, he was entirely oblivious to his own safety, though knowing that every day the Cheka net was drawn closer about him. With great difficulty he was finally prevailed upon to leave his beloved Russia, as the only way of saving his family. What a commentary on the Socialist Republic, whose bravest and truest sons must keep in hiding or forsake their native soil!
---
Life in Russia had become to me a constant torture; the need of breaking my two years’ silence was imperative. During all the summer I was in the throes of a bitter conflict between the necessity of leaving and my inability to tear myself away from what had been an ideal to me. It was like the tragic end of a great love to which one clings long after it is no more.
In the midst of my struggle there happened an event which further served to demonstrate the complete collapse of the Bolsheviki as revolutionists. It was the announcement of the return to Russia of the Tsarist General Slastchev, one of the most reactionary and brutal militarists of the old regime. He had fought against the Revolution from its very beginning and had led some of the Wrangel forces in the Crimea. He was guilty of fiendish barbarities to war prisoners and infamous as a maker of pogroms. Now Slastchev recanted and was returning to “his Fatherland.” This arch counterrevolutionist and Jew-baiter, together with several other Tsarist generals and White Guardists, was received by the Bolsheviki with military honours.

===Chinese===

小官僚的刁难与繁重的工作耗尽了她，对爱人的日夜忧惧几乎把她逼疯。可每当那男人来看她，她脸上从不见一丝愁容。

许多个夜晚，我在这对夫妇那里度过。他们与外界、与旧日的朋友完全隔绝，孤零零的，只有对暴露与死亡的恐惧形影不离。在那间阴湿的屋子里，婴儿睡熟了，我们压低嗓音，一谈几个钟头，谈乌克兰的农民，谈马赫诺运动。我的朋友对运动的每一阶段都亲身经历过，如今正把这一切写进他的马赫诺著作。他全身心扑在那部书上——它将第一次向世界讲出马赫诺和*起义军*的真相。他深深牵挂妻儿，对自己的安危却浑然不顾，尽管他知道契卡的网正一天天收紧。好说歹说，才劝动他离开心爱的俄国——那是救他全家的唯一出路。多么辛辣的注脚：社会主义共和国最勇敢、最忠贞的儿子们，要么藏身匿迹，要么背井离乡！

---

俄国的生活对我已成无休止的折磨；打破这两年沉默的需要，已刻不容缓。整个夏天，我都在离去之必要与不忍离去——不忍离开那曾是我理想的东西——之间痛苦挣扎。那像一场伟大恋爱的悲剧结局：爱情早已不在，人却还死死抱住不放。

正在挣扎中，发生了一件事，进一步证明布尔什维克作为革命者已彻底破产：沙皇将军斯拉谢夫宣布返回俄国——旧政权最反动、最残暴的军阀之一。他从革命第一天起就与之为敌，曾统领弗兰格尔的部分军队在克里米亚作战，虐杀战俘，恶行累累，又以制造排犹暴乱闻名。如今斯拉谢夫「悔悟」了，要回「祖国」了。这个头号反革命、害犹分子，同另外几个沙皇将军和白卫军一起，受到布尔什维克的军礼欢迎。

===Original===

No doubt it was just retribution that the anti-Semite had to salute the Jew Trotsky, his military superior. But to the Revolution and the Russian people the triumphal return of the imperialists was an outrage.
The old general had changed his colours but not his nature. In his letter to the officers and men of the Wrangel Army he delivered himself of the following:

> I, Slastchev Krimsky, command you to return to your Fatherland and into the fold of the Red Army. Our country needs our defence against her enemies. I command you to return.

===Chinese===

这位反犹分子不得不向他的军事上级、犹太人托洛茨基敬礼，无疑算是现世报。但对革命和俄国人民来说，帝国主义者的凯旋归来是一种凌辱。

老将军换了旗号，没换本性。他在致弗兰格尔军队官兵的信中，发表了如下宏论：

> 我，斯拉谢夫·克里姆斯基，命令你们回归祖国，回归红军的怀抱。国家需要我们保卫她、抵御她的敌人。我命令你们回来。

===Original===

As a reward for his newly fledged love of the Socialist Fatherland Slastchev “Krimsky” was commissioned to quell the Karelian peasants who demanded self-determination, and Slastchev had the opportunity of giving full play to the autocratic powers he was vested with.
Military receptions and honours for the man who had been foremost in the attempt to crush the Revolution, and imprisonment or death for the lovers of liberty! At the same time the true sons of Russia, who had defended the Revolution against every attack and had aided the Bolsheviki to political power, were made homeless by deportation to foreign lands. A more tragic debacle history has never before witnessed. The first to be deported by the “revolutionary” Government were ten Anarchists, most of them known in the international revolutionary movement as tried idealists and martyrs for their cause. Among them was Volin, a highly cultured man, a gifted writer and lecturer, who had been editor of various Anarchist publications in Europe and America. In Russia, where he returned in 1917, he helped to organize the Ukrainian Confederation of Nabat and was for a time lecturer for the Soviet Department of Education in Kharkov. Volin had been a member of an Anarchist partisan military unit that fought against Austro-German occupation, and for a considerable time he also conducted educational and cultural work in the Makhno Army. During the year 1921 he was imprisoned by the Bolsheviki and deported after the hunger strike of the Taganka Anarchists which lasted ten and a half days.
In the same group was G. Maximoff, an Anarchist of many years’ standing. Before the Revolution he had been active among the students of the Petrograd University and also among the peasants. He participated in all the revolutionary struggles beginning with the February Revolution, was one of the editors of *Golos Truda* and member of the All-Russian Secretariat of Anarcho-syndicalists. He is an able and popular writer and lecturer.
Mark Mratchny, another of the deported, has been an Anarchist since 1907. At the time when Hetman Skoropadsky ruled Ukraine with the help of German bayonets, Mratchny was a member of the Revolutionary Bureau of the students of Kharkov. He held the position of instructor in the Soviet School Department of Kharkov, and later in Siberia.

===Chinese===

为了嘉奖他对社会主义祖国新生的爱，斯拉谢夫「克里姆斯基」奉命去镇压卡累利阿要求自决的农民——斯拉谢夫于是得以把他被赋予的专制权力使个痛快。

一心扑灭革命的急先锋受军礼与荣典，热爱自由者得的却是监狱或死亡！与此同时，俄国的真儿子们——为革命挡过每一次进攻、把布尔什维克扶上政权的人——却被驱逐出境，落得无家可归。历史上从未见过比这更悲惨的大崩塌。「革命」政府驱逐的第一批是十名无政府主义者，其中多数是国际革命运动中久经考验的理想主义者和为事业受难的殉道者。其中有沃林——一位极有教养的人，天才的作家和演说家，曾任欧美多家无政府主义刊物的编辑。1917 年回国后，他协助创建乌克兰纳巴特联合会，一度在哈尔科夫苏维埃教育局任讲师。沃林参加过抗击德奥占领军的无政府主义游击队，还在马赫诺军队中长期从事教育文化工作。1921 年他被布尔什维克关押，塔甘卡监狱的无政府主义者绝食十天半之后，他被驱逐出境。

同批的 G. 马克西莫夫是多年老资格的无政府主义者。革命前，他在彼得格勒大学学生中和农民中积极活动，参加了自二月革命起的全部革命斗争，是《劳动之声》的编辑之一、无政府工团主义者全俄书记处成员，也是一位能干而受欢迎的作家和演说家。

另一位被逐者马克·姆拉奇内，1907 年起就是无政府主义者。德军刺刀扶植的斯科罗帕茨基统领乌克兰时，他是哈尔科夫学生革命局的成员，历任哈尔科夫苏维埃学校部门和后来西伯利亚的教育职位。

===Original===

He edited the *Nabat* during the period of agreement between Makhno and the Bolsheviki, and was later arrested together with the other Anarchists who had come to Kharkov for the Anarchist Conference.
Among the deported was also Yartchuk, famous as one of the leaders of the Kronstadt sailors in the uprising of July, 1917, a man who enjoyed exceptional influence among the sailors and workers and whose idealism and devotion are matters of historic record. In the group there were also several students﻿—mere youths who had participated in the Anarchist hunger-strike in the Taganka prison.
---
To remain longer in Bolshevik Russia had become unbearable. I was compelled to speak out, and decided to leave the country. Friends were making arrangements to open a sub rosa passage abroad, but just as all preparations were completed we were informed of new developments. Berlin Anarchists had made a demand upon the Soviet Government that passports be issued for Alexander Berkman, A. Shapiro, and myself, to enable us to attend the International Anarchist Congress which was to convene in Berlin in December, 1921. Whether due to that demand or for other reasons, the Soviet Government finally issued the required papers and on December 1, 1921, I left Russia in the company of Alexander Berkman and A. Shapiro. It was just one year and eleven months since I had set foot on what I believed to be the promised land. My heart was heavy with the tragedy of Russia. One thought stood out in bold relief: I must raise my voice against the crimes committed in the name of the Revolution. I would be heard regardless of friend or foe.

===Chinese===

马赫诺与布尔什维克合作的时期，他主编《纳巴特》，后来同来哈尔科夫参加无政府主义者大会的其他同志一起被捕。

被逐者中还有亚尔丘克——1917 年 7 月起义中喀琅施塔得水兵的著名领袖之一，在水兵和工人中享有非凡影响，其理想主义与献身精神是有史可稽的。这批人里还有几名学生——不过是一些参加了塔甘卡监狱无政府主义者绝食的青年。

---

再留在布尔什维克的俄国已不可忍受。我必须说话，于是决定离境。朋友们正在安排一条秘密出国的路，一切就绪之际，我们获悉了新的进展：柏林的无政府主义者向苏维埃政府提出要求，为亚历山大·伯克曼、A. 夏皮罗和我签发护照，使我们得以出席 1921 年 12 月在柏林召开的国际无政府主义者大会。不知是由于这一要求还是别的原因，苏维埃政府终于签发了所需的证件。1921 年 12 月 1 日，我偕亚历山大·伯克曼和 A. 夏皮罗离开了俄国。距我踏上那片我信为应许之地的土地，恰好一年零十一个月。我的心为俄国的悲剧沉沉下坠。只有一个念头赫然挺立：我必须为以革命之名犯下的罪行发出控诉的声音。无论友敌，我必被听见。

## XV The Trade Unions / 十五、工会
===Original===

It was the month of June and the time of our departure was approaching. Petrograd seemed more beautiful than ever; the white nights had come﻿—almost broad daylight without its glare, the mysterious soothing white nights of Petrograd. There were rumours of counterrevolutionary danger and the city was guarded against attack. Martial law prevailing, it was forbidden to be out on the streets after 1 a.m., even though it was almost daylight. Occasionally special permits were obtained by friends and then we would walk through the deserted streets or along the banks of the dark Neva, discussing in whispers the perplexing situation. I sought for some outstanding feature in the blurred picture﻿—the Russian Revolution, a huge flame shooting across the world illuminating the black horizon of the disinherited and oppressed﻿—the Revolution, the new hope, the great spiritual awakening. And here I was in the midst of it, yet nowhere could I see the promise and fulfilment of the great event. Had I misunderstood the meaning and nature of revolution? Perhaps the wrong and the evil I have seen during those five months were inseparable from a revolution. Or was it the political machine which the Bolsheviki have created﻿—is that the force which is crushing the Revolution? If I had witnessed the birth of the latter I should now be better able to judge. But apparently I arrived at the end﻿—the agonizing end of a people. It is all so complex, so impenetrable, a *tupik*, a blind alley, as the Russians call it. Only time and earnest study, aided by sympathetic understanding, will show me the way out. Meanwhile, I must keep up my courage and﻿—away from Petrograd, out among the people.
Presently the long-awaited moment arrived. On June 30, 1920, our car was coupled to a slow train called “Maxim Gorky,” and we pulled out of the Nikolayevski station, bound for Moscow.
In Moscow there were many formalities to go through with. We thought a few days would suffice, but we remained two weeks. However, our stay was interesting. The city was alive with delegates to the Second Congress of the Third International; from all parts of the world the workers had sent their comrades to the promised land, revolutionary Russia, the first republic of the workers.

===Chinese===

时值六月，我们动身的日子近了。彼得格勒比以往任何时候都美；白夜来了——近乎白昼却没有烈日的那种光，彼得格勒神秘而安详的白夜。反革命危险的传闻不断，全城严防攻击。戒严令下，凌晨一点后不得上街，尽管天几乎还是亮的。偶尔朋友们弄到特别通行证，我们便在空寂的街上、在黝黑的涅瓦河岸散步，低声讨论着令人困惑的局势。我在模糊的图景中寻找一个凸出的焦点——俄国革命，一道射向世界的巨大火焰，照亮被剥夺者和被压迫者的黑色地平线——革命，新的希望，伟大的精神觉醒。而我正身处其中，却到处看不到这伟大事件的许诺与实现。是我误解了革命的意义与本质吗？也许我五个月来所见的不义与罪恶，本就与革命不可分。抑或是布尔什维克造出的那台政治机器——碾碎革命的正是它？倘若我目击的是革命诞生，如今评判起来会更有把握。可我显然到得太晚——赶上的是一个民族弥留的痛苦尽头。这一切太复杂、太不可测，一个*tupik*、一条死胡同——俄国人这么叫它。唯有时间加上认真的研究，辅以同情的理解，会给我指出路。眼下，我必须鼓起勇气，并且——离开彼得格勒，到人民中间去。

盼望已久的时刻终于到了。1920 年 6 月 30 日，我们的车厢挂上一列叫「马克西姆·高尔基」的慢车，驶出尼古拉耶夫斯基车站，开往莫斯科。

在莫斯科要办的手续很多。本以为几天够了，结果待了两个星期。不过此行很有意思：城里到处是来参加第三国际第二次代表大会的代表。世界各地的工人把同志们派到应许之地——革命的俄国，第一个工人共和国。

===Original===

Among the delegates there were also Anarchists and syndicalists who believed as firmly as I did six months previously that the Bolsheviki were the symbol of the Revolution. They had responded to the Moscow call with enthusiasm. Some of them I had met in Petrograd and now they were eager to hear of my experiences and learn my opinions. But what was I to tell them, and would they believe me if I did? Would I have believed any adverse criticism before I came to Russia? Besides, I felt that my views regarding the Bolsheviki were still too unformed, too vague, a conglomeration of mere impressions. My old values had been shattered and so far I have been unable to replace them. I could therefore not speak on the fundamental questions, but I did inform my friends that the Moscow and Petrograd prisons were crowded with Anarchists and other revolutionists, and I advised them not to content themselves with the official explanations but to investigate for themselves. I warned them that they would be surrounded by guides and interpreters, most of them men of the Cheka, and that they would not be able to learn the facts unless they made a determined, independent effort.
There was considerable excitement in Moscow at the time. The Printers’ Union had been suppressed and its entire managing board sent to prison. The Union had called a public meeting to which members of the British Labour Mission were invited. There the famous Socialist Revolutionist Chernov had unexpectedly made his appearance. He severely criticised the Bolshevik regime, received an ovation from the huge audience of workers, and then vanished as mysteriously as he had come. The Menshevik Dan was less successful. He also addressed the meeting, but he failed to make his escape: he landed in the Cheka. The next morning the Moscow *Pravda* and the *Izvestia* denounced the action of the Printers’ Union as counterrevolutionary, and raged about Chernov having been permitted to speak. The papers called for exemplary punishment of the printers who dared defy the Soviet Government.
The Bakers’ Union, a very militant organization, had also been suppressed, and its management replaced by Communists. Several months before, in March, I had attended a convention of the bakers. The delegates impressed me as a courageous group who did not fear to criticise the Bolshevik regime and present the demands of the workers.

===Chinese===

代表中也有无政府主义者和工团主义者，他们同六个月前的我一样，坚信布尔什维克是革命的象征。他们怀着热忱响应了莫斯科的召唤。有几个我在彼得格勒见过，如今他们急于听我的经历、我的看法。可我能对他们说什么呢？说了他们信吗？我来俄国之前，若有逆耳的批评，我信吗？何况我自觉对布尔什维克的看法还太不成形、太模糊，只是一堆印象的堆积；旧的价值碎了，至今没换上新的。因此根本的问题我谈不了，但我确实告诉朋友们：莫斯科和彼得格勒的监狱里关满了无政府主义者和其他革命者；我劝他们不要满足于官方的解释，要自己去调查。我警告他们：向导和译员会包围他们——多半是契卡的人——除非拿出决绝的独立劲头，否则摸不到实情。

当时莫斯科很不太平。印刷工会被取缔，整个理事会进了监狱。该工会召集了一次公开大会，请了英国劳工代表团的成员。会上，那位著名的社会革命党人切尔诺夫出人意料地现身，痛批布尔什维克政权，赢得满场工人的欢呼，随即又神秘地失踪了，如他来时一样。孟什维克丹恩就没这么走运：他也上台讲了话，却没能脱身——进了契卡。第二天早晨，莫斯科《真理报》和《消息报》痛斥印刷工会的行径为反革命，为切尔诺夫居然被允许发言而大光其火，并要求对敢于违抗苏维埃政府的印刷工人惩一儆百。

面包工人工会——一个很强的战斗性组织——也被取缔，领导班子换上了共产党人。几个月前的三月，我出席过面包工人的一次代表大会。代表们给我的印象是一群有勇气的人，不怕批评布尔什维克政权，敢把工人的要求摆出来。

===Original===

I wondered then that they were permitted to continue the conference, for they were outspoken in their opposition to the Communists. “The bakers are ‘*Shkurniki*,’ ”14 I was told; “they always instigate strikes, and only counterrevolutionists can wish to strike in the workers’ Republic.” But it seemed to me that the workers could not follow such reasoning. They did strike. They even committed a more heinous crime: they refused to vote for the Communist candidate, electing instead a man of their own choice. This action of the bakers was followed by the arrest of several of their more active members. Naturally the workers resented the arbitrary methods of the Government.
Later I met some of the bakers and found them much embittered against the Communist Party and the Government. I inquired about the condition of their union, telling them that I had been informed that the Russian unions were very powerful and had practical control of the industrial life of the country. The bakers laughed. “The trade unions are the lackeys of the Government,” they said; “they have no independent function, and the workers have no say in them. The trade unions are doing mere police duty for the Government.” That sounded quite different from the story told by Melnichansky, the chairman of the Moscow Trade Union Soviet, whom I had met on my first visit to Moscow.
On that occasion he had shown me about the trade union headquarters known as the Dom Soyusov, and explained how the organization worked. 7,000,000 workers were in the trade unions, he said; all trades and professions belonged to it. The workers themselves managed the industries and owned them. “The building you are in now is also owned by the unions,” he remarked with pride; “formerly it was the House of the Nobility.” The room we were in had been used for festive assemblies and the great nobles sat in crested chairs around the table in the centre. Melnichansky showed me the secret underground passage hidden by a little turntable, through which the nobles could escape in case of danger. They never dreamed that the workers would some day gather around the same table and sit in the beautiful hall of marble columns. The educational and cultural work done by the trade unions, the chairman further explained, was of the greatest scope.

===Chinese===

我当时就纳闷，这样的会怎么能开得下去——他们反共话说得太直了。「面包工人是『*自私鬼*』14，」我被告知，「他们总爱煽动罢工；在工人共和国里想罢工的，只能是反革命。」可依我看，工人们听不进这套逻辑。他们照样罢工，甚至还犯下更重的罪：拒绝投票给共产党候选人，选了自己中意的人。此举招来几名活跃分子被捕。工人自然怨恨政府的专横手段。

后来我见到几个面包工人，发现他们对党和政府怨愤已深。我问起工会的状况，说有人告诉我，俄国工会势力强大，实际执掌着国家的工业生活。面包工人们笑了。「工会是政府的走卒，」他们说，「没有任何独立职能，工人在里面没有发言权。工会干的纯粹是替政府当警察的差。」这话同梅尔尼昌斯基的说法大相径庭——他是莫斯科工会苏维埃的主席，我初访莫斯科时见过他。

那次他领我参观了工会总部「工会大厦」，讲解了组织如何运作。他说，工会里有七百万工人，各行各业全在册。工人自己管理工业并拥有工业。「您现在待的这栋楼也是工会的产业，」他自豪地说，「从前是贵族会堂。」我们所在的那间厅，旧日专办节庆盛会，大贵族们围着中央长桌坐在家徽椅里。梅尔尼昌斯基指给我看藏在一张小转台下的秘密地下通道——贵族们遇险时可以由此逃生。他们做梦也想不到，有朝一日工人会围坐同一张桌子，坐进这间大理石圆柱的华厅。主席又讲解说，工会的教育文化工作规模宏大。

===Original===

“We have our workers’ colleges and other cultural institutions giving courses and lectures on various subjects. They are all managed by the workers. The unions own their own means of recreation, and we have access to all the theatres.” It was apparent from his explanation that the trade unions of Russia had reached a point far beyond anything known by labour organizations in Europe and America.
A similar account I had heard from Tsiperovitch, the chairman of the Petrograd trade unions, with whom I had made my first trip to Moscow. He had also shown me about the Petrograd Labour Temple, a beautiful and spacious building where the Petrograd unions had their offices. His recital also made it clear that the workers of Russia had at last come into their own.
But gradually I began to see the other side of the medal. I found that like most things in Russia the trade union picture had a double facet: one paraded before foreign visitors and “investigators,” the other known by the masses. The bakers and the printers had recently been shown the other side. It was a lesson of the benefits that accrued to the trade unions in the Socialist Republic.
In March I had attended an election meeting arranged by the workers of one of the large Moscow factories. It was the most exciting gathering I had witnessed in Russia﻿—the dimly lit hall in the factory club rooms, the faces of the men and women worn with privation and suffering, the intense feeling over the wrong done them, all impressed me very strongly. Their chosen representative, an Anarchist, had been refused his mandate by the Soviet authorities. It was the third time the workers gathered to reelect their delegate to the Moscow Soviet, and every time they elected the same man. The Communist candidate opposing him was Semashko, the Commissar of the Department of Health. I had expected to find an educated and cultured man. But the behaviour and language of the Commissar at that election meeting would have put a hod-carrier to shame. He raved against the workers for choosing a non-Communist, called anathema upon their heads, and threatened them with the Cheka and the curtailment of their rations. But he had no effect upon the audience except to emphasize their opposition to him, and to arouse antagonism against the party he represented. The final victory, however, was with Semashko.

===Chinese===

「我们有工人大学和其他文化机构，开各科课程和讲座，全由工人管理。工会自办娱乐场所，所有剧院我们都能进。」听他这一讲，俄国工会显然已远远超出欧美劳工组织的水平。

类似的说法我也从齐佩罗维奇那里听过——彼得格勒工会的主席，我头一趟去莫斯科时与他同行。他还领我参观过彼得格勒劳动宫，一座漂亮宽敞的大楼，彼得格勒各工会的办公处。听他一讲，俄国工人也总算翻身当家了。

但我渐渐看到了勋章的另一面。我发现，同俄国的多数事物一样，工会这幅画有两副面孔：一副给外国来客和「考察者」检阅，另一副归大众知道。面包工人和印刷工人最近就领教了另一面——这是社会主义共和国工会所得实惠的一课。

三月里，我参加过莫斯科一家大工厂工人安排的选举大会。那是我在俄国见过的最激动的集会——工厂俱乐部里灯光昏暗，男男女女的脸被匮乏与苦难磨蚀，人们对所受不公的强烈情绪，都给我极深的印象。他们选出的代表是无政府主义者，被苏维埃当局拒绝了当选证书。这已是工人第三次集会改选他们出席莫斯科苏维埃的代表，每次选中的都是同一个人。与他对阵的共产党候选人是谢马什科，卫生人民委员。我原以为会见到一位有教养的文化人，可这位委员在选举大会上的言行，连个搬砖的都不如。他咆哮着骂工人选了非共产党人，咒他们遭天谴，拿契卡和削减口粮吓唬他们。可他除了把听众的反感烧得更旺、把人们推向他所代表的党的对立面，毫无效果。不过，最后的胜利属于谢马什科。

===Original===

The workers’ choice was repudiated by the authorities and later even arrested and imprisoned. That was in March. In May, during the visit of the British Labour Mission, the factory candidate together with other political prisoners declared a hunger strike, which resulted in their liberation.
The story told me by the bakers of their election experiences had the quality of our own Wild West during its pioneer days. Chekists with loaded guns were in the habit of attending gatherings of the unions and they made it clear what would happen if the workers should fail to elect a Communist. But the bakers, a strong and militant organization, would not be intimidated. They declared that no bread would be baked in Moscow unless they were permitted to elect their own candidate. That had the desired effect. After the meeting the Chekists tried to arrest the candidate-elect, but the bakers surrounded him and saw him safely home. The next day they sent their ultimatum to the authorities, demanding recognition of their choice and threatening to strike in case of refusal. Thus the bakers triumphed and gained an advantage over their less courageous brothers in the other labour organizations of minor importance. In starving Russia the work of the bakers was as vital as life itself.

===Chinese===

工人选出的人被当局否决，后来索性被捕入狱。那是三月的事。五月，英国劳工代表团访问期间，那位工厂候选人同其他政治犯一起宣布绝食，方才换得获释。

面包工人对我讲的选举经历，颇有我们拓荒年代「狂野西部」的味道。契卡分子荷枪赴会已成惯例，把不选共产党人的后果交代得明明白白。可面包工人这个坚强好斗的组织不吃吓：他们宣告，不让他们选自己的人，莫斯科就一块面包也烤不出来。这一招立竿见影。会后契卡想抓当选人，面包工人们把他围在中间，护送他平安到家。第二天他们向当局发出最后通牒：要求承认他们的选择，否则罢工。面包工人就这样赢了，在那些无足轻重的行业组织里不如他们勇敢的弟兄们面前，占了上风。在饥馑的俄国，面包工人的活计，同生命本身一样攸关。

## XXIX Travelling Salesmen of the Revolution / 二十九、革命的行商
===Original===

Great preparations were being made by the Communists for the Third Congress of the Third International and the First Congress of the Red Trade Union International. A preliminary committee had been organized in the summer of 1920, while delegates from various countries were in Moscow. How much the Bolsheviki depended upon the First Congress of the Red Trade Union International was apparent from a remark of an old Communist. “We haven’t the workers in the Third International,” he said; “unless we succeed in welding together the proletariat of the world into the R.T.U.I., the Third International cannot last very long.”
The Hôtel de Luxe, renovated the previous year, became the foreign guest house of the Third International and was put in festive attire. The delegates began to arrive in Moscow.
During my stay in Russia I came across three classes of visitors who came to “study the Revolution.” The first category consisted of earnest idealists to whom the Bolsheviki were the symbol of the Revolution. Among them were many emigrants from America who had given up everything they possessed to return to the promised land. Most of these became bitterly disappointed after the first few months and sought to get out of Russia. Others, who did not come as Communists, joined the Communist Party for selfish reasons and did in Rome as the Romans do. There were also the Anarchist deportees who came not of their own choice. Most of them strained every effort to leave Russia after they realized the stupendous deception that had been imposed on the world.
In the second class were journalists, newspapermen, and some adventurers. They spent from two weeks to two months in Russia, usually in Petrograd or Moscow, as the guests of the Government and in charge of Bolshevik guides. Hardly any of them knew the language and they never got further than the surface of things. Yet many of them have presumed to write and lecture authoritatively about the Russian situation. I remember my astonishment when I read in a certain London daily that the teachings of Jesus were “being realized in Russia.” A preposterous falsehood of which none but a charlatan could be guilty. Other writers were not much nearer the truth.

===Chinese===

共产党人正为第三国际第三次代表大会和红色工会国际第一次代表大会张罗盛大筹备。筹备委员会早在 1920 年夏、各国代表云集莫斯科时就已成立。布尔什维克何等指望红色工会国际的成立大会，从一位老共产党人的话里可见一斑：「第三国际里我们并没有工人，」他说，「除非把全世界无产者熔进红色工会国际，第三国际撑不了多久。」

豪华旅馆——头一年刚翻修过——成了第三国际的外宾馆，装点得喜气洋洋。各国代表陆续抵达莫斯科。

旅俄期间，我遇到过三类来「考察革命」的客人。第一类是诚恳的理想主义者，在他们眼里布尔什维克就是革命的象征。其中有许多从美国回来的侨民，抛下全部家产奔赴应许之地。这些人多数不出几个月便痛感幻灭，想法子离开俄国；另一些本来不是共产党人的，出于私利入了党，入乡随俗去了。还有被迫放逐而来的无政府主义者，一旦认清那场强加给全世界的弥天骗局，便拼命想离开俄国。

第二类是记者、报人和一些冒险家。他们在俄国待两星期到两个月，通常在彼得格勒或莫斯科，做政府的客人，由布尔什维克的向导陪同。几乎没人懂语言，看到的从不超过表层。可其中许多人竟摆出权威的架势著书演讲，大谈俄国局势。记得我在伦敦一家日报上读到「耶稣的教导正在俄国实现」时，何等惊愕。荒谬绝伦的谎言——只有江湖骗子才说得出。别的作者离真相也不更近些。

===Original===

If they were at all critical of the Bolsheviki they were so at the expense of the whole Russian people, whom they charged with being “crude, primitive savages, too illiterate to grasp the meaning of the Revolution.” According to these writers it was the Russian people who imposed upon the Bolsheviki their despotic and cruel methods. It did not occur to those so-called investigators that the Revolution was made by those primitive and illiterate people, and not by the present rulers in the Kremlin. Surely they must have possessed some quality which enabled them to rise to revolutionary heights﻿—a quality which, if properly directed, would have prevented the wreck and ruin of Russia. But that quality has persistently been overlooked by Bolshevik apologists who sacrifice all truth in their determination to find extenuating circumstances for the mess made by the Bolsheviki. A few wrote with understanding of the complex problems and with sympathy for the Russian people. But their voice was ineffectual in the popular craze that Bolshevism had become.
The third category﻿—the majority of the visitors, delegates, and members of various commissions﻿—infested Russia to become the agents of the ruling Party. These people had every opportunity to see things as they were, to get close to the Russian people, and to learn from them the whole terrible truth. But they preferred to side with the Government, to listen to its interpretation of causes and effects. Then they went forth to misrepresent and to lie deliberately in behalf of the Bolsheviki, as the Entente agents had lied and misrepresented the Russian Revolution.
Nor did the sincere Communists realize the disgrace of the situation﻿—not even Angelica Balabanova. Yet she had good judgment of character and knew how to appraise the people who flocked to Russia. Her experience with Mrs. Clare Sheridan was characteristic. The lady had been smuggled into Russia before Moscow realized that she was the cousin of Winston Churchill. She was obsessed by the desire “to sculp” prominent Communists. She had also begged Angelica to sit for her. “Lenin, Trotsky, and other leaders are going to; aren’t you?” she pleaded. Angelica, who hated sensationalism in any form, resented the presence in Russia of these superficial visitors.

===Chinese===

他们即便批评布尔什维克，也拿整个俄国人民垫背，说他们是「粗野、原始的蛮子，文盲得连革命的意义都领会不了」。照这些作者的说法，是俄国人民把专制残暴的手段强加给了布尔什维克。这些所谓的考察者没想到：革命恰恰是那些原始的文盲干出来的，不是克里姆林宫现在的统治者们干的。他们必定有一种品质，使他们能奋起达到革命的高度——这品质若引导得当，本可免掉俄国的覆灭。可布尔什维克的辩护士们执意要为这伙人的烂摊子找开脱，不惜牺牲一切真相，对那种品质视而不见。也有少数作者带着对复杂问题的理解和同情写作，但在布尔什维主义成了流行狂热的年头，他们的声音无济于事。

第三类人——访客、代表和各色委员会成员的大多数——麇集俄国，是要做执政党的代理人。这些人本有充分的机会看清事物的本来面目，走近俄国人民，从他们口中听全那可怕的真相。可他们宁愿站在政府一边，听政府对因果的一套解释，然后出去颠倒黑白、蓄意为布尔什维克撒谎，一如协约国的走狗们当年为诋毁俄国革命而撒谎那样。

就连真诚的共产党人也没意识到这局面的可耻——连安热莉卡·巴拉班诺娃起初也没意识到。其实她看人颇有眼力，深知涌到俄国来的都是些什么货色。她同克莱尔·谢里登夫人的打交就是典型一例。这位夫人在莫斯科方面发现她是温斯顿·丘吉尔的表妹之前，被偷偷带进了俄国。她满脑子想「塑」共产党要人的半身像，也恳求安热莉卡给她当模特。「列宁、托洛茨基和其他领袖都肯的，您还不肯吗？」她劝道。安热莉卡憎恶一切形式的耸动，对这路浅薄的访客深为反感。

===Original===

“I asked her,” she afterward related, “if she would have thought of ‘sculping’ Lenin three years ago when the English Government denounced him as a German spy. Lenin did not make the Revolution. The Russian people made it. I told this Mrs. Sheridan that she would do better to ‘sculp’ Russian workingmen and women who were the real heroes of the Revolution. I know she did not like what I said. But I don’t care. I can’t stand people to whom the Russian struggle is mere copy for poor imitations or cheap display.”
Now the new delegates were beginning to arrive. They were royally welcomed and fêted. They were taken to show schools, children’s homes, colonies, and model factories. It was the traditional Potemkin villages29 that were shown the visitors. They were graciously received and “talked to” by Lenin and Trotsky, treated to theatres, concerts, ballets, excursions, and military parades. In short, nothing was left undone to put the delegates into a frame of mind favourable to the great plan that was to be revealed to them at the Red Trade Union and the Third International Congresses. There were also continuous private conferences where the delegates were subjected to a regular third degree, Lozovsky﻿—prominent Bolshevik labour leader﻿—and his retinue seeking to ascertain their attitude to the Third International, the dictatorship of the proletariat, and similar subjects. Here and there was a delegate who refused to divulge the instructions of his organization on the ground that he was pledged to report only to the Congress. But such naive people reckoned without their host. They soon found themselves ostracized and at the Congress they were given no opportunity to make themselves heard effectively.
The majority of the delegates were more pliable. They learned quickly that pledges and responsibilities were considered bourgeois superstitions. To show their ultra-radicalism they quickly divested themselves of them. They became the echoes of Zinoviev, Lozovsky, and other leaders.
The American delegates to the Red Trade Union International were most conspicuous by their lack of personality. They accepted without question every proposition and suggestion of the Chair.

===Chinese===

「我问她，」安热莉卡后来讲，「三年前英国政府骂列宁是德国间谍的时候，她可曾想到去『塑』列宁？列宁没有制造革命，是俄国人民干的。我告诉这位谢里登夫人，她不如去『塑』俄国男女工人——他们才是革命的真英雄。我知道她不爱听。可我不在乎。那些把俄国的挣扎只当作拙劣模仿和廉价展览素材的人，我受不了。」

如今新代表们纷纷抵达，受着王侯般的欢迎与宴请，被领去看「秀」学校、儿童之家、营舍和模范工厂。给客人们看的，是老一套的波将金村29。他们受列宁和托洛茨基亲切接见、「赐谈」，看戏、听音乐会、观芭蕾、游览、观阅兵。总之，无所不用其极，好把代表们放进一种心境，乐于接受即将在红色工会国际和第三国际大会上向他们揭晓的伟大计划。此外还有没完没了的私下谈话，代表们被施以正规的疲劳审讯——罗佐夫斯基——布尔什维克的著名劳工头目——及其一班人，要摸清各人对第三国际、无产阶级专政等问题的态度。偶尔也有代表拒绝泄露本组织的训令，理由是他只对大会负责。可这种天真的人打错了算盘：很快便遭孤立，到了大会上，根本得不到让人听见的机会。

多数代表圆通得多。他们很快学会了：承诺和责任都是资产阶级迷信，为表现自己的超激进，三下两下便抖落干净。他们成了季诺维也夫、罗佐夫斯基和其他领袖的回声。

赴红色工会国际的的美国代表们，最醒目之处就是毫无个性：主席台的一切提议和暗示，他们照单全收。

===Original===

The most flagrant intrigues and political machinations and brazen suppression of those who would not be cajoled or bullied into blind adherence found ready support by the American Communist crew and the aides they had brought with them.
